## Authentication/Authorization Exploration Report

Based on my thorough exploration of this NestJS + React monorepo, here's what exists and what's missing:

### WHAT EXISTS

**1. Backend Project Foundation**
- NestJS v11 + Fastify adapter (strict TypeScript mode)
- Well-organized modular structure with proper import aliases
- Environment configuration system in place (EnvConfigService, env validations)
- Health check endpoints (liveness/readiness via Terminus)
- Swagger/OpenAPI setup utilities
- Global validation pipe with class-validator
- CORS enabled (origin: true, credentials: true)

**2. Project Directories (Empty but Planned)**
- `/backend/src/guards/` - prepared directory for auth guards (.gitkeep)
- `/backend/src/decorators/` - prepared directory for custom decorators (.gitkeep)
- `/backend/src/middlewares/` - prepared directory for middlewares (.gitkeep)
- `/backend/src/dto/` - prepared directory for DTOs (.gitkeep)

**3. Development Infrastructure**
- Jest testing (unit + e2E) with 95% coverage requirements
- ESLint v9 with custom rules (no relative imports, no console.log)
- Prettier formatting
- Husky pre-commit hooks
- Comprehensive env variable validation system

### WHAT'S MISSING

**1. Core Authentication Libraries**
- No passport, passport-jwt, passport-local, or other strategies
- No @nestjs/jwt or jwt-decode
- No OAuth/OIDC libraries (passport-google-oauth20, etc.)
- No bcrypt or password hashing libraries
- No @nestjs/passport package

**2. Database/ORM**
- No TypeORM, Prisma, Drizzle, or any ORM installed
- No database connection setup
- No user entity/model defined
- No migrations system

**3. Authentication-Related Environment Variables**
- `.env.example` contains only basic app config (PORT, NODE_ENV, etc.)
- Missing: JWT_SECRET, JWT_EXPIRY, DB_URL, OAUTH providers, session config, etc.

**4. Authentication Modules & Services**
- No auth module
- No user module/service
- No auth controller
- No JWT strategy
- No session/token management service
- No password reset/email verification flow

**5. Security Features**
- No rate limiting (@nestjs/throttler or express-rate-limit)
- No helmet for HTTP headers
- No request validation for auth endpoints
- No logout/token blacklist system
- No refresh token implementation

**6. Frontend**
- Frontend directory exists but appears empty (scaffolding not completed per CLAUDE.md)
- No frontend package.json or auth context/hooks

### FILE STRUCTURE REFERENCES

**Existing modules:**
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/no-name-proj/backend/src/modules/app/app.module.ts`
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/no-name-proj/backend/src/modules/env-config/` (env configuration)

**Package.json dependencies:**

### KEY OBSERVATIONS

1. **Ready for Auth Implementation** - The project has excellent scaffolding and conventions that will make adding auth straightforward
2. **Environment System Ready** - The 5-step env variable process is already established and can accommodate auth secrets/tokens
3. **No Database Yet** - You'll need to choose and configure an ORM before implementing user models

### NEXT STEPS RECOMMENDATION

1. Select a database (TypeORM/Prisma) and ORM
2. Add auth libraries (passport, jwt, bcrypt)

Project state: NestJS + Fastify backend with good scaffolding but **no database, no ORM, no auth libraries yet**. Clean slate for auth.

Before I propose approaches, first question:

**Do you need a database for user persistence, or is this purely OAuth-based (stateless)?**

Options:
- **A) Full user model** — Store users in a DB, link OAuth accounts, support profile data, roles, etc.
- **B) Stateless OAuth** — Just verify the OAuth token on each request, no local user storage
- **C) Lightweight hybrid** — Minimal user record in DB (id, email, provider) just to track who's logged in, no rich profile

Which fits your use case?

Good context. So we're building a multi-tenant B2B app with company-scoped data.

Next question:

**Which database do you want to use?**

Options:
- **A) PostgreSQL** — Best fit for relational multi-tenant data (companies, users, roles). Most common with NestJS
- **B) MongoDB** — Document-based, more flexible schema but weaker for relational queries
- **C) No preference** — I'll recommend based on your use case

> DEVELOPER

lets use postgresql with read and write replicas

> AGENT

Good choice. Read/write replicas from the start — that tells me you're planning for scale.

Next question:

**Which ORM/query layer?**

Options:
- **A) Prisma** — Type-safe, great DX, schema-first migrations. Some limitations with read replicas (needs `@prisma/extension-read-replicas` or manual datasource switching)
- **B) TypeORM** — Native NestJS integration, decorator-based entities, built-in replication config. More mature but weaker type safety
- **C) Drizzle** — Lightweight, SQL-like syntax, excellent TypeScript types, easy multi-datasource setup. Newer but gaining traction fast
- **D) No preference**

My lean: **Drizzle or TypeORM**. Both handle read/write replicas natively without extensions. Prisma's replica support is bolted on and adds complexity.

## Comprehensive Database Module Exploration Report

I've thoroughly explored `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/modules/db/` and related configuration files. Here's the complete analysis of patterns, configurations, and setup:

### 1. KNEX.JS CONFIGURATION

**Files:**
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/modules/db/db.module.ts`
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/knexfile.ts`
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/constants/db.constant.ts`

**Key Configuration Details:**

**Read/Write Replica Setup:**
- Two separate Knex instances via `nest-knexjs`
- `KNEX_READ_CONNECTION`: Uses `DATABASE_URL_READ_REPLICA` (read-only replica)
- `KNEX_WRITE_CONNECTION`: Uses `DATABASE_URL` (primary write connection)

**Connection Pooling:**
```typescript
// Read connection pool configuration:
pool: {
  min: 10,
  max: 30,
  acquireTimeoutMillis: 10_000,
  idleTimeoutMillis: 30_000
}

// Write connection:
// No explicit pool config (uses Knex defaults)
```

**Client Configuration:**
- Both use `mysql2` client
- MySQL version: 8.0
- Charset: utf8mb4
- Connection via URI (environment variable)

**Table Name Constants:**
All 15+ tables use centralized constants (never raw strings):
- `TABLE_NAME_STYLES`
- `TABLE_NAME_STYLES_COLORS`
- `TABLE_NAME_STYLES_HASH`
- `TABLE_NAME_STYLE_COPURCHASES` (FBT data)
- `TABLE_NAME_CATEGORIES_CORRELATIONS`
- `TABLE_NAME_TRENDING_PRODUCTS`
- `TABLE_NAME_PRODUCT_ANNUAL_SALES`
- And 8+ more in `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/constants/db.constant.ts`

---

### 2. NESTJS INTEGRATION (db.module.ts)

**File:** `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/modules/db/db.module.ts`

**Module Structure:**
- Imports both KnexModule instances via `KnexModule.forRoot()` with distinct names
- Registers 26 total providers (13 Models + 13 Services) and exports all
- Each model is paired with its service

**Provider Registration Pattern:**
Models and Services registered together:
```typescript
providers: [
  StylesHashService, StylesHashModel,
  CondensedFeaturesService, CondensedFeaturesModel,
  // ... repeats for all 13 domains
],
exports: [
  // Same as providers
]
```

---

### 3. BASE REPOSITORY PATTERNS

**Dependency Injection in Models:**
```typescript
constructor(
  @InjectConnection(KNEX_READ_CONNECTION) private readonly knexRead: Knex,
  @InjectConnection(KNEX_WRITE_CONNECTION) private readonly knexWrite: Knex,
)
```

**Core Methods in All Models:**

1. **Single Record Reads:**
   - `findById()` / `findByStyleId()`
   - Always chains `.whereNull('deleted_at')`
   - Validates output via `plainToInstance()` + `validate()`

2. **Bulk Reads:**
   - `findByIds()` / `findByStyleIds()`
   - Uses `.whereIn()` for multiple IDs
   - Parallel Promise.all() validation

3. **Query Filtering & Soft Deletes:**
   - All reads automatically filter soft-deleted rows: `.whereNull('deleted_at')`
   - Soft deletes set both `deleted_at` and `updated_at` to `knexWrite.fn.now()`
   - Never hard-delete; use soft deletes exclusively

4. **Batch Operations:**
   - `bulkInsert()` / `bulkUpsert()` in batches of 1000
   - Uses `.onConflict().merge()` for upserts

5. **Advanced Queries:**
   - `unionAll()` for combining results (e.g., finding pairs in both directions)
   - CTE with `.with()` for window functions
   - `.orderByRaw()` for COUNT(*) and complex ordering
   - Joins with `.join()` passing callback for complex predicates

**Example from StyleCopurchaseModel (complex query):**
```typescript
// Union two queries to find pairs in both directions
const queryA = this.knexRead(this.tableName)
  .select('style_id_b as paired_style_id', 'copurchase_count')
  .where({ style_id_a: styleId })
  .whereNull('deleted_at');

const results = await this.knexRead
  .unionAll([queryA, queryB])
  .orderBy('copurchase_count', 'desc')
  .limit(limit);
```

---

### 4. DTO AND INTERFACE CONVENTIONS

**Location Structure:**
```
<domain>/
  ├── types/
  │   ├── <domain>.base.interface.ts      # Full row shape
  │   ├── <domain>.create.interface.ts    # Omit<IBase, '_id' | timestamps>
  │   └── <domain>.update.interface.ts    # Partial<Omit<...>>
  ├── dto/
  │   ├── <domain>.base.dto.ts            # class-validator decorators
  │   ├── <domain>.create.dto.ts          # OmitType
  │   └── <domain>.update.dto.ts          # PartialType + OmitType
  └── <domain>.service.ts
```

**Interface Pattern (IStyleBase example):**
```typescript
export interface IStyleBase {
  _id: string;                               // UUID primary key
  style_id: number;
  // ... domain fields
  created_at: Date;
  updated_at: Date;
  deleted_at: Date | null;                  // Soft delete marker
}

export type IDomainCreate = Omit<IStyleBase, '_id' | 'created_at' | 'updated_at' | 'deleted_at'>;
export type IDomainUpdate = Partial<Omit<IStyleBase, '_id' | 'created_at' | 'updated_at' | 'deleted_at'>>;
```

**DTO Pattern with class-validator:**
```typescript
export class BaseStyleDto {
  @IsUUID()
  @IsNotEmpty()
  _id: string;

  @IsOptional()
  @IsArray()
  @IsInt({ each: true })
  @Transform(({ value }: { value: number[] | string }) => {
    if (Array.isArray(value)) return value;
    if (typeof value === 'string') {
      try {
        return JSON.parse(value) as number[];
      } catch {
        return [];
      }
    }
    return [];
  })
  categories_ids: number[] | null;
  
  // ... more fields
}

// OmitType removes _id, created_at, updated_at, deleted_at
export class CreateStyleDto extends OmitType(BaseStyleDto, [
  '_id', 'created_at', 'updated_at', 'deleted_at',
] as const) {}

export class UpdateStyleDto extends PartialType(
  OmitType(BaseStyleDto, ['_id', 'created_at', 'updated_at', 'deleted_at'] as const),
) {}
```

**Key DTO Patterns:**
- Use `@Transform` for JSON parsing (JSON columns stored as strings)
- Use `@Transform` for type coercion (tinyint booleans → boolean)
- `@IsOptional()` precedes type validators for nullable fields
- `@IsNotEmpty()` for non-nullable fields

### 5. MODEL LAYER CONVENTIONS

**File Example:** `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/modules/db/styles/models/style.model.ts`

**Standard Structure:**
```typescript
@Injectable()
export class DomainModel {
  private readonly logger = new Logger(DomainModel.name);
  private readonly tableName = TABLE_NAME_X;

  constructor(
    @InjectConnection(KNEX_READ_CONNECTION) private readonly knexRead: Knex,
    @InjectConnection(KNEX_WRITE_CONNECTION) private readonly knexWrite: Knex,
  ) {}

  // All methods wrapped in try/catch with logging
  async methodName(): Promise<ReturnType> {
    try {
      // Validate input DTO if writing
      const dto = plainToInstance(BaseDto, input);
      const errors = await validate(dto);
      if (errors.length > 0) {
        throw new Error(`Validation failed: ${this.formatValidationErrors({ errors })}`);
      }

      // Execute query
      const result = await this.knexRead(this.tableName)...;

      // Validate output DTO
      const outputDto = plainToInstance(BaseDto, result);
      const outputErrors = await validate(outputDto);
      if (outputErrors.length > 0) {
        throw new Error(...);
      }

      this.logger.debug('Success message');
      return instanceToPlain(result) as ReturnType;
    } catch (error) {
      this.logger.error(`Failed: ${(error as Error).message}`);
      throw error;
    }
  }

  private formatValidationErrors({ errors }: { errors: ValidationError[] }): string {
    return errors
      .map((error) => `${error.property}: ${Object.values(error.constraints ?? {}).join(', ')}`)
      .join('; ');
  }
}
```

**Key Patterns:**
- All methods have try/catch with error logging
- Input validation: `plainToInstance()` + `validate()`
- Output validation: same pattern
- Return `instanceToPlain()` to convert back to plain objects
- Log levels: `debug` for success, `warn` for not-found, `error` for failures

**Advanced Upsert Pattern with Transactions:**
```typescript
async upsertStyle({
  data,
}: {
  data: Partial<IStyleCreate> & { style_id: IStyleBase['style_id'] };
}): Promise<IStyleBase> {
  // Validate input first
  const dto = plainToInstance(BaseStyleDto, data);
  const errors = await validate(dto, { skipMissingProperties: true });
  if (errors.length > 0) throw new Error(...);

  const upsertedStyle: IStyleBase = await this.knexWrite.transaction(async (trx) => {
    const existing = await trx<IStyleBase>(this.tableName)
      .where({ style_id: data.style_id })
      .whereNull('deleted_at')
      .first();

    if (existing) {
      // Update existing
      const updateData: Record<string, unknown> = {
        updated_at: trx.fn.now(),
      };
      // Handle JSON fields specially
      Object.keys(data).forEach((key) => {
        if (key === 'categories_ids' || key === 'sizes') {
          updateData[key] = JSON.stringify(data[key as keyof typeof data] ?? []);
        } else {
          updateData[key] = data[key as keyof typeof data];
        }
      });
      await trx(this.tableName)
        .where({ style_id: data.style_id })
        .whereNull('deleted_at')
        .update(updateData);
    } else {
      // Insert new
      const _id = randomUUID();
      const insertData = {
        _id,
        ...data,
        categories_ids: JSON.stringify(data.categories_ids ?? []),
        created_at: trx.fn.now(),
        updated_at: trx.fn.now(),
      };
      await trx(this.tableName).insert(insertData);
    }

    return await trx<IStyleBase>(this.tableName)
      .where({ style_id: data.style_id })
      .whereNull('deleted_at')
      .first();
  });

  return this.parseStyleData({ styleData: plainData });
}
```

**Multi-Model Transactions:**
```typescript
async saveCompleteStyle({
  styleData,
  colors,
  defaultUnitPrices,
}: {
  styleData: Partial<IStyleCreate> & { style_id: IStyleBase['style_id'] };
  colors: Omit<IStyleColorCreate, 'style_id'>[];
  defaultUnitPrices: Omit<IDefaultUnitPriceCreate, 'style_id'>[];
}): Promise<void> {
  await this.knexWrite.transaction(async (trx) => {
    await this.upsertStyleWithTransaction({ trx, data: styleData });
    for (const color of colors) {
      await this.styleColorModel.upsertStyleWithTransaction({ trx, data: { ...color, style_id: styleData.style_id } });
    }
    for (const priceData of defaultUnitPrices) {
      await this.defaultUnitPriceModel.upsertWithTransaction({ trx, data: { ...priceData, style_id: styleData.style_id } });
    }
  });
}
```

**Methods Accepting External Transactions:**
```typescript
async upsertStyleWithTransaction({
  trx,
  data,
}: {
  trx: Knex.Transaction;
  data: Partial<IStyleCreate> & { style_id: IStyleBase['style_id'] };
}): Promise<IStyleBase> {
  // Use trx instead of this.knexWrite for all queries
  const existing = await trx<IStyleBase>(this.tableName)
    .where({ style_id: data.style_id })
    .whereNull('deleted_at')
    .first();
  // ... rest of logic
}
```

### 6. SERVICE LAYER CONVENTIONS

**File Example:** `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/modules/db/styles/styles.service.ts`

**Thin Facade Pattern:**
```typescript
@Injectable()
export class StylesService {
  constructor(private readonly styleModel: StyleModel) {}

  async getStyleById({ styleId }: { styleId: IStyleBase['style_id'] }): Promise<IStyleBase | null> {
    return this.styleModel.findByStyleId({ styleId });
  }

  @LogExecution({
    paramFormatter: (params) => params,
    responseFormatter: () => '',
  })
  async getStylesInStockByIds({ styleIds }: { styleIds: IStyleBase['style_id'][] }): Promise<IStyleBase[]> {
    return this.styleModel.getStylesInStockByIds({ styleIds });
  }

  async saveCompleteStyle({
    styleData,
    colors,
    defaultUnitPrices,
  }: {
    styleData: Partial<IStyleCreate> & { style_id: IStyleBase['style_id'] };
    colors: Omit<IStyleColorCreate, 'style_id'>[];
    defaultUnitPrices: Omit<IDefaultUnitPriceCreate, 'style_id'>[];
  }): Promise<void> {
    return this.styleModel.saveCompleteStyle({ styleData, colors, defaultUnitPrices });
  }
}
```

**Service Patterns:**
- Minimal business logic; delegates to model
- Apply `@LogExecution` decorator for performance tracking
- Use same parameter/return types as model methods
- No validation (model handles it)

### 7. MIGRATIONS SYSTEM

**File Example:** `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/migrations/20251125120000_create_styles_mv_table.ts`

**Pattern:**
```typescript
import { Logger } from '@nestjs/common';
import { TABLE_NAME_STYLES, TABLE_NAME_STYLES_MV } from '@constants/db.constant';
import type { Knex } from 'knex';

const logger = new Logger('20251125120000_create_styles_mv_table.ts');

export async function up(knex: Knex): Promise<void> {
  logger.log('Creating styles_mv table');
  
  await knex.transaction(async (trx) => {
    const tableExists = await trx.schema.hasTable(TABLE_NAME_STYLES_MV);
    
    if (!tableExists) {
      await trx.schema.createTable(TABLE_NAME_STYLES_MV, (table) => {
        table.uuid('_id').primary().notNullable();
        table.bigint('style_id').notNullable();
        
        // Regular columns
        table.string('name', 255).nullable();
        table.timestamp('created_at').defaultTo(trx.fn.now()).notNullable();
        table.timestamp('updated_at').defaultTo(trx.fn.now()).notNullable();
        table.timestamp('deleted_at').nullable();
        
        // Indexes
        table.unique(['style_id'], { predicate: trx.whereNull('deleted_at') });
        table.index(['primary_category_id', 'deleted_at']);
        
        // Foreign key
        table.foreign('style_id', 'fk_styles_mv_style_id')
          .references('style_id')
          .inTable(TABLE_NAME_STYLES)
          .onDelete('CASCADE');
      });
      
      logger.log('Successfully created styles_mv table');
    } else {
      logger.log('styles_mv table already exists, skipping');
    }
  });
}

export async function down(knex: Knex): Promise<void> {
  logger.log('Dropping styles_mv table');
  
  await knex.transaction(async (trx) => {
    await trx.schema.dropTableIfExists(TABLE_NAME_STYLES_MV);
  });
}
```

**Migration Commands:**
```bash
npm run migrate:make <name>        # Generate timestamped file in src/migrations/
npm run migrate                    # Run all pending migrations
npm run migrate:up                 # Run next single migration
npm run migrate:down               # Rollback last migration
npm run migrate:prod               # Run in production
```

**Migration Rules:**
- Always wrap in `knex.transaction(async (trx) => { ... })`
- Never edit existing migration; create new one instead
- Use table name constants (never raw strings)
- Check `tableExists` before creating to make migration idempotent
- Use conditional predicates for soft delete indexes: `{ predicate: trx.whereNull('deleted_at') }`
- Use `knexfile.ts` in project root for migration config

### 8. KNEX QUERY BUILDER PATTERNS

**Soft Delete Queries:**
```typescript
// Always filter soft-deleted rows
await this.knexRead(this.tableName)
  .where({ style_id: styleId })
  .whereNull('deleted_at')
  .first();

// Soft delete (never hard delete)
await this.knexWrite(this.tableName)
  .where({ style_id: styleId })
  .whereNull('deleted_at')
  .update({
    deleted_at: this.knexWrite.fn.now(),
    updated_at: this.knexWrite.fn.now(),
  });
```

**Complex Queries:**
```typescript
// Union All for bidirectional relationships
const queryA = this.knexRead(this.tableName)
  .select('style_id_b as paired_style_id', 'copurchase_count')
  .where({ style_id_a: styleId })
  .whereNull('deleted_at');

const queryB = this.knexRead(this.tableName)
  .select('style_id_a as paired_style_id', 'copurchase_count')
  .where({ style_id_b: styleId })
  .whereNull('deleted_at');

const results = await this.knexRead
  .unionAll([queryA, queryB])
  .orderBy('copurchase_count', 'desc')
  .limit(limit);

// Window functions with CTE
const query = this.knexRead
  .with(
    'copurchases',
    baseQuery
      .select(
        this.knexRead.raw(
          'ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY primary_category_id ORDER BY copurchase_count DESC) AS rn',
        ),
      ),
  )
  .select('paired_style_id', 'copurchase_count', 'primary_category_id')
  .from('copurchases')
  .where('rn', 1);

// Raw SQL for complex expressions
query.orderByRaw('COUNT(*) desc');
query.orderByRaw('CASE WHEN style_type != ? THEN 0 ELSE 1 END', [seedStyleType]);

// Joins with callback
.join(TABLE_NAME_STYLES_MV + ' as mv', function () {
  this.on('mv.style_id', '=', 'pairs.paired_style_id').andOnNull('mv.deleted_at');
});

// Bulk upsert with conflict resolution
await this.knexWrite(this.tableName)
  .insert(batch)
  .onConflict(['primary_category_id', 'edge_id'])
  .merge(['edge_weight', 'updated_at', 'deleted_at']);
```

**Batch Processing:**
```typescript
const batchSize = 1000;
for (let i = 0; i < dataToInsert.length; i += batchSize) {
  const batch = dataToInsert.slice(i, i + batchSize);
  
  await this.knexWrite(this.tableName).insert(batch);
  
  this.logger.debug(
    `Processed batch ${Math.floor(i / batchSize) + 1}/${Math.ceil(dataToInsert.length / batchSize)}`,
  );
}
```

**Insert with Generated UUID:**
```typescript
import { randomUUID } from 'node:crypto';

const _id = randomUUID();
await trx(this.tableName).insert({
  _id,
  ...data,
  created_at: trx.fn.now(),
  updated_at: trx.fn.now(),
});
```

### 11. KEY TAKEAWAYS FOR REPLICATION

1. **Always use read/write separation:** Inject both `KNEX_READ_CONNECTION` and `KNEX_WRITE_CONNECTION` in every model.

2. **Soft deletes everywhere:** No hard deletes. Always `.whereNull('deleted_at')` on reads and `.update({ deleted_at: knex.fn.now() })` for deletes.

3. **UUID + Timestamps in Application Code:**
   - Primary key: `randomUUID()` from `node:crypto`
   - Timestamps: `trx.fn.now()` (database time)

4. **Validation at Boundaries:**
   - Input: `plainToInstance()` + `validate()` before database writes
   - Output: same pattern before returning to callers
   - Use DTO with `@Transform` for JSON parsing and type coercion

5. **Thin Service Layer:** Services are minimal facades that delegate to models; models contain all database logic.

6. **Transaction Pattern:** For multi-model operations, pass `trx: Knex.Transaction` through methods and use it instead of `knexWrite`.

7. **Batch Operations:** Process in chunks of 1000 to avoid memory issues with large datasets.

8. **Logging:** Use `Logger` at model level with `debug`, `warn`, and `error` levels. Apply `@LogExecution` at service level for performance tracking.

---

All files and their absolute paths have been documented above. This pattern is production-grade and proven in the recommendations-service codebase.

Next question:

**Session management — how should we handle the authenticated session after OAuth login?**

Options:
- **A) JWT (access + refresh tokens)** — Stateless, stored client-side. Access token short-lived (15min), refresh token longer (7d). Standard for SPAs.
- **B) Server-side sessions** — Session stored in Redis/DB, session ID in httpOnly cookie. Simpler revocation but requires session store.
- **C) JWT with server-side revocation list** — Hybrid: JWT for performance, but track revoked tokens in Redis for logout/security.

My lean: **A** — JWT pair. Fits a React SPA well, no session store needed at this stage, and your current backend is stateless Fastify.

Next question:

**Where to store the revocation list — Redis or PostgreSQL?**

Options:
- **A) Redis** — Fast, TTL-based auto-expiry (tokens clean themselves up), purpose-built for this. Adds an infrastructure dependency.
- **B) PostgreSQL table** — No new infra, but you need a cleanup job to purge expired tokens. Slower lookups on every request.
- **C) Decide later** — Abstract behind an interface, implement with PG first, swap to Redis when needed.

My lean: **A** if you already plan to use Redis anywhere else in the stack. **C** if you want to keep infra minimal for now.

Here are 3 approaches for the auth architecture:

---

### Approach A: Passport.js + NestJS Guards (Classic)

- `@nestjs/passport` with Google/GitHub strategies
- Passport handles OAuth dance, NestJS guards protect routes
- Session stored in Redis via `connect-redis`
- JWT issued after OAuth callback

**Pros:** Battle-tested, huge ecosystem, tons of examples
**Cons:** Passport is a heavy abstraction for just 2 providers. Middleware-oriented design feels awkward in NestJS's decorator-based architecture. Passport v0.7 still has quirks with Fastify.

---

### Approach B: Manual OAuth + Custom Guards (Lean)

- Implement OAuth flows directly (HTTP calls to Google/GitHub token endpoints via `@nestjs/axios` or `fetch`)
- Custom NestJS guards + decorators for auth/roles
- JWT signing via `@nestjs/jwt`
- Redis sessions via `ioredis`

**Pros:** No Passport overhead, full control, cleaner Fastify compatibility, easier to debug. Fewer dependencies.
**Cons:** More code to write upfront. You own the OAuth token exchange logic.

---

### Approach C: Arctic + Custom Guards (Modern)

- [Arctic](https://arcticjs.dev/) — lightweight OAuth 2.0 library, provider-specific helpers for Google/GitHub. Zero dependencies.
- Same custom guards/decorators as B
- Same JWT + Redis session pattern

**Pros:** Cleaner than raw HTTP calls, no Passport bloat, built for modern runtimes. Handles provider quirks (PKCE, token refresh) without you writing it.
**Cons:** Newer library, smaller community than Passport.

---

### My recommendation: **B or C**

Passport is overkill for 2 OAuth providers on Fastify. Between B and C — **C (Arctic)** saves you from writing OAuth token exchange boilerplate while staying lightweight. But if you prefer zero external auth libs, **B** is fine too since the OAuth flow is ~50 lines per provider.

## Complete Redis Caching Pattern Analysis

Based on my thorough exploration of `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/modules/cache/`, here's the complete pattern for Redis configuration with NestJS, including read/write separation, clustering, and locking mechanisms:

---

### 1. **CACHE MODULE SETUP** (`cache.module.ts`)

The module configures:

- **Two separate Redis connections** (read/write separation):
  - `REDIS_WRITE_CLIENT`: Primary write connection from `REDIS_URL`
  - `REDIS_READ_CLIENT`: Read replica connection from `REDIS_READ_URL`
  
- **Two Keyv cache managers** for abstraction:
  - `CACHE_WRITE_MANAGER`: Uses write URL
  - `CACHE_READ_MANAGER`: Uses read URL

- **Keyv configuration** (via `@keyv/redis`):
  ```
  - namespace: `${SERVER_NAME}:${NODE_ENV}` (e.g., "reco-ms:development")
  - useUnlink: true (faster async deletion vs UNLINK)
  - keyPrefixSeparator: ':'
  ```

- **ioredis options** for write client:
  ```typescript
  retryStrategy: (times) => Math.min(times * 50, 2000)
  maxRetriesPerRequest: 3
  enableReadyCheck: true
  connectTimeout: 10000
  lazyConnect: false
  reconnectOnError: (err) => err.message.includes('READONLY')
  ```

- **ioredis options** for read client:
  ```typescript
  readOnly: true  // Enforces read-only mode
  (same retry strategy as write)
  ```

---

### 2. **CACHE SERVICE** (`cache.service.ts`)

**Core Methods:**

- `get<T>()` - Reads from cache with consistency control:
  - `ConsistencyLevel.EVENTUAL` → reads from replica (if healthy)
  - `ConsistencyLevel.STRONG` → reads from master
  - Falls back to master on replica failure

- `set()` - Writes with TTL support:
  - Tracks write timestamps in `recentWrites` Map
  - Supports optional `ttl` parameter (milliseconds)

- `has()` - Key existence check (via `get()`)

- `deleteOne()` - Single key deletion

- `deleteAll()` - Clear all keys (with 100ms delay between manager calls)

- `incrementByOne()` - Atomic increment via Redis INCR command

**Locking Mechanism (Redlock pattern):**

- `withLock<T>()` - Wrapper for operations with automatic lock release:
  ```typescript
  await cacheService.withLock({
    lockKey: 'my-lock',
    operation: async () => { /* your code */ },
    ttl: 5000  // default 5s
  })
  ```

- `acquireLock()` - Manual lock acquisition
- `releaseLock()` - Manual lock release

**Redlock Configuration:**
```typescript
new Redlock([redisWriteClient], {
  driftFactor: 0.01,
  retryCount: 10,
  retryDelay: 200,
  retryJitter: 200,
})
```

**Smart Read/Write Logic:**

- `shouldReadFromMaster()` checks:
  1. Consistency level requirement
  2. Replica health status
  3. Recent write window (1000ms) - routes to master if key written recently
  4. `recentWrites` Map tracks last write time for each key
  5. Automatic cleanup of entries older than 2 seconds

**Health Checks:**

- Listens to `redisReadClient` 'error' and 'ready' events
- Sets `isReadReplicaHealthy` flag
- Automatic fallback on replica error

---

### 3. **CONSTANTS & ENV VARIABLES**

**Environment Variables** (`env-variables.constant.ts`):
```typescript
REDIS_URL          // Primary write connection
REDIS_READ_URL     // Read replica connection
NODE_ENV            // For namespacing
SERVER_NAME         // For namespacing
```

**Provider Constants** (`common.constant.ts`):
```typescript
REDIS_READ_CLIENT   // 'REDIS_READ_CLIENT'
REDIS_WRITE_CLIENT  // 'REDIS_WRITE_CLIENT'
CACHE_WRITE_MANAGER // 'CACHE_WRITE_MANAGER'
CACHE_READ_MANAGER  // 'CACHE_READ_MANAGER'
```

**Cache Namespace Enum** (`cache.interface.ts`):
```typescript
enum Namespaces {
  RELATED_ITEMS_MODEL = 'related-items-model',
  FBT_MODEL = 'fbt-model',
  MMS_COMPLEMENTS = 'mms-complements',
  MMS_ALTERNATIVES = 'mms-alternatives',
  HEALTH_CHECK = 'health-check',
  METRICS = 'metrics',
  CURATED_ADDONS = 'curated-addons',
}
```

**.env.example** entries:
```
REDIS_URL=redis://localhost:6377
REDIS_READ_URL=redis://localhost:6378
RELATED_PRODUCTS_CACHE_TTL=129600000  # 36 hours
CURATED_ADDONS_CACHE_TTL=18000000    # 5 hours
```

---

### 4. **NPM PACKAGES** (`package.json`)

**Required Dependencies:**
```json
"ioredis": "^5.10.0"              // Redis client
"@keyv/redis": "^5.1.6"           // Keyv Redis adapter
"@nestjs/cache-manager": "^3.1.0" // NestJS cache abstraction
"redlock": "^5.0.0-beta.2"        // Distributed locking
```

---

### 5. **ERROR HANDLING**

**Custom CacheError interface:**
```typescript
interface CacheError extends Error {
  code: 'CONNECTION_FAILED' | 'KEY_NOT_FOUND' | 'OPERATION_FAILED'
  originalError?: Error
}
```

**Error scenarios:**
- Connection failures → throw `CONNECTION_FAILED`
- Operation failures → throw `OPERATION_FAILED`
- Replica fails → log warning, fallback to master with `STRONG` consistency
- Master fails → propagate error

---

### 6. **USAGE PATTERN** (from metrics.service.ts)

```typescript
@Injectable()
export class MetricsService {
  constructor(private readonly cacheService: CacheService) {}

  async incrementDailyCounter({ metricType }: { metricType: string }) {
    const today = new Date().toISOString().split('T')[0];
    const key = `${metricType}:${today}`;
    await this.cacheService.incrementByOne({ 
      namespace: Namespaces.METRICS, 
      key 
    });
  }
}
```

---

### 7. **KEY ARCHITECTURAL DECISIONS**

1. **Dual Keyv managers** - Allows different Redis URLs for read/write without changing client code
2. **Recent writes tracking** - Prevents stale reads within 1-second window after writes
3. **Eventual vs Strong consistency** - Trade-off control at call site
4. **Redlock on write client only** - Locks only need to block writes, not reads
5. **Health checks on read replica** - Graceful degradation when replica fails
6. **Async-friendly unlink** - `useUnlink: true` prevents blocking during deletes
7. **Namespacing** - Isolates different domains and environments automatically

---

### **File Locations**

- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/modules/cache/cache.module.ts`
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/modules/cache/cache.service.ts`
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/constants/common.constant.ts`
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/constants/env-variables.constant.ts`
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/src/interfaces/cache.interface.ts`
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/.env.example`
- `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/recommendations-service/test/cache.module.e2e-spec.ts`

This pattern is production-ready with comprehensive error handling, health monitoring, and lock management. Ready for replication in your session store module.

> AGENT

Got it. Your Redis setup uses `ioredis` with read/write separation, `@keyv/redis` for caching, `redlock` for distributed locks, and health-based fallback. We'll replicate this pattern for session storage.

Let me present the design section by section. Tell me after each if it looks right.

---

## Section 1: Data Model

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ users                                    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ _id          UUID PK                     │
│ email        VARCHAR(255) UNIQUE NOT NULL │
│ name         VARCHAR(255)                │
│ avatar_url   VARCHAR(512)                │
│ role         ENUM('admin','member')      │
│ company_id   UUID FK → companies._id    │  ← NULL for members (use junction)
│ created_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ updated_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ deleted_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ oauth_accounts                           │
├─────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ _id          UUID PK                     │
│ user_id      UUID FK → users._id        │
│ provider     ENUM('github','google')     │
│ provider_id  VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL       │
│ email        VARCHAR(255)                │
│ access_token TEXT (encrypted)            │
│ created_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ updated_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ deleted_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│                                          │
│ UNIQUE(provider, provider_id)            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ companies                                │
├─────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ _id          UUID PK                     │
│ name         VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL       │
│ created_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ updated_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ deleted_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ company_members                          │
├─────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ _id          UUID PK                     │
│ user_id      UUID FK → users._id        │
│ company_id   UUID FK → companies._id    │
│ role         ENUM('admin','member')      │
│ created_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ updated_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ deleted_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│                                          │
│ UNIQUE(user_id, company_id)              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ invitations                              │
├─────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ _id          UUID PK                     │
│ company_id   UUID FK → companies._id    │
│ email        VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL       │
│ role         ENUM('admin','member')      │
│ token        VARCHAR(255) UNIQUE         │
│ expires_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ accepted_at  TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ created_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ updated_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
│ deleted_at   TIMESTAMPTZ                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key decisions:**
- `company_members` is the junction table — members can belong to multiple companies, admins are 1:1 (enforced in app logic)
- `oauth_accounts` separate from `users` — supports multiple providers per user + the "prompt to link" flow
- `invitations` table tracks pending invites with expiry tokens
- `users.company_id` is the admin's "owned" company — quick lookup without joining. NULL for pure members.
- All tables follow your existing patterns: UUID PK, soft deletes, timestamps

Does this look right?

> DEVELOPER

oauth_accounts and users should be separate or can be 1 table? explain

> AGENT

Two valid approaches:

**Separate tables (current proposal):**
- One user can have multiple OAuth accounts (GitHub + Google)
- Clean normalization — user data lives in `users`, provider data in `oauth_accounts`
- The "prompt to link" flow you chose requires looking up existing accounts by provider — separate table makes this a simple query
- Adding a third provider later = just insert a row, no schema change

**Merged into one table:**
- Simpler queries for login (one table hit)
- But: if a user links both GitHub and Google, you need either duplicate user rows or multiple `provider_*` column pairs (`github_id`, `google_id`, `github_token`, `google_token`...)
- Adding a new provider = schema migration to add columns
- Conflict detection ("this email exists with a different provider") becomes messier

**Verdict:** Separate tables. Your requirements demand it — multiple providers per user + account linking prompt. Merging would fight the data model.

Stay with the current design?

> DEVELOPER

stay with current design..1 question..why we have access tocket in the db if our plan is to store sessions in the redis?

> AGENT

Different tokens:

- **`oauth_accounts.access_token`** — the token from GitHub/Google API. You need this to **call their APIs** (fetch repos, Jira data, user profile updates). Without storing it, you'd have to re-authenticate every time you want to pull data from GitHub/Jira.

- **Session in Redis** — your app's session. Tracks "this user is logged in." Has nothing to do with GitHub/Google APIs.

They serve different purposes. The OAuth access token is a credential for external APIs, not for your app's auth.

**However** — if you're only using GitHub/Google for *login identity* and won't call their APIs from your backend, then you don't need to store it. You'd only need `provider` + `provider_id` to identify the user.

## Section 2: Auth Flow

```
┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌───────────┐     ┌───────┐
│  React   │     │ Backend  │     │ GitHub/   │     │ Redis │
│  SPA     │     │ NestJS   │     │ Google    │     │       │
└────┬─────┘     └────┬─────┘     └─────┬─────┘     └───┬───┘
     │                │                  │               │
     │ GET /auth/github                  │               │
     │───────────────>│                  │               │
     │                │  redirect to provider            │
     │<───────────────│                  │               │
     │                │                  │               │
     │ ──── browser redirect ──────────>│               │
     │                │                  │               │
     │ callback with code               │               │
     │───────────────>│                  │               │
     │                │  exchange code   │               │
     │                │────────────────>│               │
     │                │  access_token   │               │
     │                │<────────────────│               │
     │                │  fetch profile  │               │
     │                │────────────────>│               │
     │                │  user data      │               │
     │                │<────────────────│               │
     │                │                  │               │
     │                │  ┌─────────────────────────┐    │
     │                │  │ Lookup oauth_accounts   │    │
     │                │  │ by provider+provider_id │    │
     │                │  │                         │    │
     │                │  │ EXISTS + SAME USER?     │    │
     │                │  │  → login                │    │
     │                │  │                         │    │
     │                │  │ EXISTS + DIFF USER?     │    │
     │                │  │  → prompt to link       │    │
     │                │  │                         │    │
     │                │  │ NOT EXISTS + email match?│    │
     │                │  │  → prompt to link       │    │
     │                │  │                         │    │
     │                │  │ NOT EXISTS + no match?  │    │
     │                │  │  → new signup flow      │    │
     │                │  └─────────────────────────┘    │
     │                │                  │               │
     │                │  create session  │               │
     │                │──────────────────────────────>│
     │                │                  │               │
     │  JWT (access + refresh)           │               │
     │<───────────────│                  │               │
     │                │                  │               │
```

**On every authenticated request:**
1. Validate JWT signature + expiry
2. Check session exists in Redis (key: `session:{user_id}:{session_id}`)
3. If session gone → 401, force re-login

**Token refresh:**
1. Client sends refresh token
2. Backend validates it, checks session in Redis
3. Issues new access token (1hr), rotates refresh token (30d)

**Logout:**
- Delete session from Redis → JWT becomes invalid immediately

## Section 3: Module Architecture

```
backend/src/modules/
├── auth/
│   ├── auth.module.ts
│   ├── auth.controller.ts        # OAuth endpoints, login, logout, refresh
│   ├── auth.service.ts           # OAuth flow orchestration, token issuance
│   ├── strategies/
│   │   ├── github.strategy.ts    # Arctic GitHub provider
│   │   └── google.strategy.ts    # Arctic Google provider
│   ├── guards/
│   │   ├── auth.guard.ts         # JWT + session validation
│   │   └── roles.guard.ts        # Role-based access (admin/member)
│   ├── decorators/
│   │   ├── current-user.decorator.ts   # Extract user from request
│   │   ├── roles.decorator.ts          # @Roles('admin')
│   │   └── public.decorator.ts         # Skip auth for specific routes
│   ├── dto/
│   ├── types/
│   └── constants/
│
├── session/
│   ├── session.module.ts
│   ├── session.service.ts        # Redis session CRUD, uses your cache pattern
│   └── types/
│
├── user/
│   ├── user.module.ts
│   ├── user.service.ts
│   ├── user.model.ts             # Knex queries for users table
│   ├── dto/
│   └── types/
│
├── oauth-account/
│   ├── oauth-account.module.ts
│   ├── oauth-account.service.ts
│   ├── oauth-account.model.ts    # Knex queries for oauth_accounts
│   ├── dto/
│   └── types/
│
├── company/
│   ├── company.module.ts
│   ├── company.service.ts
│   ├── company.model.ts
│   ├── dto/
│   └── types/
│
├── invitation/
│   ├── invitation.module.ts
│   ├── invitation.service.ts
│   ├── invitation.model.ts
│   ├── dto/
│   └── types/
│
├── cache/                         # Redis module (replicated from recommendations-service)
│   ├── cache.module.ts
│   └── cache.service.ts
│
└── db/
    └── db.module.ts               # Knex read/write connections (pg client)
```

**Key points:**
- `session` module is separate from `auth` — session is infrastructure, auth is business logic
- `cache` module replicates your existing ioredis pattern with read/write separation + redlock
- Models follow your Knex pattern: inject `KNEX_READ_CONNECTION` / `KNEX_WRITE_CONNECTION`, soft deletes, UUID PKs, DTO validation
- Guards are in `auth/guards/` not in the global `guards/` dir — they're auth-specific

## Section 4: API Endpoints

```
# OAuth
GET  /auth/github              → Redirect to GitHub OAuth
GET  /auth/github/callback     → Handle GitHub callback
GET  /auth/google              → Redirect to Google OAuth
GET  /auth/google/callback     → Handle Google callback

# Session
POST /auth/refresh             → Refresh access token
POST /auth/logout              → Destroy session

# Account linking (when conflict detected)
POST /auth/link                → Link OAuth account to existing user

# Signup (new company creation)
POST /auth/signup/company      → Create company after first OAuth login

# User
GET  /users/me                 → Current user profile + active company

# Company
GET  /companies/:id            → Company details (admin: full, member: limited)

# Invitations (admin only)
POST /companies/:id/invitations         → Invite engineer by email
GET  /companies/:id/invitations         → List pending invitations
DELETE /companies/:id/invitations/:id   → Revoke invitation

# Invitation acceptance (public, token-based)
GET  /invitations/accept?token=xxx      → Validate invite, redirect to OAuth
```

**Auth flow detail:**
- OAuth callback returns a **temporary code** (not JWT) to the frontend
- Frontend exchanges code for JWT via a POST — avoids tokens in URL/browser history
- On conflict (email exists with different provider), callback returns a linking prompt token instead

Does this look right?

> DEVELOPER

ok

## Section 5: Session Structure in Redis

```
Key:    session:{user_id}:{session_id}
TTL:    30 days (matches refresh token)

Value:  {
  user_id:      "uuid",
  session_id:   "uuid",
  role:         "admin" | "member",
  company_id:   "uuid" | null,      // active company context
  provider:     "github" | "google",
  ip:           "x.x.x.x",
  user_agent:   "...",
  created_at:   "ISO timestamp",
  last_active:  "ISO timestamp"
}
```

**Session operations:**
- **Create** — on login, write to Redis with 30d TTL
- **Validate** — on every request, auth guard checks key exists
- **Refresh** — on token refresh, update `last_active`
- **Destroy** — on logout, delete key
- **Destroy all** — admin can kill all sessions for a user (password compromise, etc.)

**Pattern for "destroy all":**
- Scan keys matching `session:{user_id}:*` and delete
- Alternatively, maintain a set `sessions:{user_id}` listing all session IDs for O(1) lookup

**Company context switching (for members in multiple companies):**
- Member calls `POST /users/me/company` with `company_id`
- Updates `company_id` in the Redis session
- Next access token issued includes the new company context

Does this look right?

> DEVELOPER

ok

> AGENT

## Section 6: Security Considerations

**OAuth:**
- CSRF protection via `state` parameter in OAuth flow (Arctic handles this)
- PKCE for Google (Arctic supports it natively)
- OAuth tokens encrypted at rest in `oauth_accounts.access_token` via AES-256

**JWT:**
- Signed with RS256 (asymmetric) or HS256 (symmetric). Recommend **HS256** — simpler, single backend, no key distribution needed.
- Access token payload: `{ user_id, session_id, role, company_id }` — minimal claims
- Refresh token: opaque UUID stored alongside session in Redis, not a JWT

**Transport:**
- Cookies: `httpOnly`, `secure`, `sameSite: strict` for refresh token
- Access token: returned in response body, stored in memory (not localStorage) on frontend
- CORS: restrict to your frontend domain

**Rate limiting:**
- `/auth/*` endpoints: strict rate limit (e.g., 10 req/min per IP) via `@nestjs/throttler`
- Token refresh: 5 req/min per user

**Invitation tokens:**
- Cryptographically random (48 bytes, base64url)
- 72hr expiry
- Single use — mark `accepted_at` on use

That's the full design. Does it look right, or anything you'd change?

> DEVELOPER

       |\n| updated_at   | TIMESTAMPTZ           | NOT NULL, DEFAULT now()         |\n| deleted_at   | TIMESTAMPTZ           | NULL                            |\n\n- UNIQUE(provider, provider_id)\n- Supports multiple providers per user (account linking)\n\n### companies\n\n| Column     | Type         | Constraints             |\n|------------|--------------|-------------------------|\n| _id        | UUID         | PK                      |\n| name       | VARCHAR(255) | NOT NULL                |\n| created_at | TIMESTAMPTZ  | NOT NULL, DEFAULT now() |\n| updated_at | TIMESTAMPTZ  | NOT NULL, DEFAULT now() |\n| deleted_at | TIMESTAMPTZ  | NULL                    |\n\n### company_members\n\n| Column     | Type                  | Constraints                  |\n|------------|-----------------------|------------------------------|\n| _id        | UUID                  | PK                           |\n| user_id    | UUID                  | FK → users._id, NOT NULL     |\n| company_id | UUID                  | FK → companies._id, NOT NULL |\n| role       | ENUM(admin,member)    | NOT NULL                     |\n| created_at | TIMESTAMPTZ           | NOT NULL, DEFAULT now()      |\n| updated_at | TIMESTAMPTZ           | NOT NULL, DEFAULT now()      |\n| deleted_at | TIMESTAMPTZ           | NULL                         |\n\n- UNIQUE(user_id, company_id)\n- Admins: 1:1 with company (enforced in app logic)\n- Members: can belong to multiple companies\n\n### invitations\n\n| Column      | Type                | Constraints                  |\n|-------------|---------------------|------------------------------|\n| _id         | UUID                | PK                           |\n| company_id  | UUID                | FK → companies._id, NOT NULL |\n| email       | VARCHAR(255)        | NOT NULL                     |\n| role        | ENUM(admin,member)  | NOT NULL                     |\n| token       | VARCHAR(255)        | UNIQUE, NOT NULL             |\n| expires_at  | TIMESTAMPTZ         | NOT NULL                     |\n| accepted_at | TIMESTAMPTZ         | NULL                         |\n| created_at  | TIMESTAMPTZ         | NOT NULL, DEFAULT now()      |\n| updated_at  | TIMESTAMPTZ         | NOT NULL, DEFAULT now()      |\n| deleted_at  | TIMESTAMPTZ         | NULL                         |\n\n## Auth Flow\n\n### OAuth Login Sequence\n\n1. Frontend redirects to `GET /auth/{provider}`\n2. Backend generates state param, redirects to provider\n3. Provider redirects back to `GET /auth/{provider}/callback` with code\n4. Backend exchanges code for access token via Arctic\n5. Backend fetches user profile from provider\n6. Account resolution:\n   - **Existing oauth_account, same user** → login\n   - **Existing oauth_account, different user** → prompt to link\n   - **No oauth_account, email matches existing user** → prompt to link\n   - **No oauth_account, no email match** → new signup flow\n7. Create session in Redis\n8. Return JWT (access + refresh) to frontend\n\n### Token Exchange Security\n\n- OAuth callback returns a temporary authorization code to frontend (not JWT)\n- Frontend exchanges code for JWT via POST — avoids tokens in URL/browser history\n\n### Token Refresh\n\n1. Client sends refresh token (from httpOnly cookie)\n2. Backend validates refresh token, checks session exists in Redis\n3. Issues new access token (1hr), rotates refresh token (30d)\n\n### Request Authentication\n\nOn every authenticated request:\n1. Validate JWT signature + expiry\n2. Check session exists in Redis (key: `session:{user_id}:{session_id}`)\n3. If session gone → 401, force re-login\n\n### Logout\n\n- Delete session key from Redis → JWT becomes invalid immediately\n\n## Session Structure (Redis)\n\n```\nKey:    session:{user_id}:{session_id}\nTTL:    30 days\n\nValue:  {\n  user_id:      \"uuid\",\n  session_id:   \"uuid\",\n  role:         \"admin\" | \"member\",\n  company_id:   \"uuid\" | null,\n  provider:     \"github\" | \"google\",\n  ip:           \"x.x.x.x\",\n  user_agent:   \"...\",\n  created_at:   \"ISO timestamp\",\n  last_active:  \"ISO timestamp\"\n}\n```\n\n### Session Operations\n\n- **Create** — on login, write to Redis with 30d TTL\n- **Validate** — on every request, auth guard checks key exists\n- **Refresh** — on token refresh, update `last_active`\n- **Destroy** — on logout, delete key\n- **Destroy all** — scan keys `session:{user_id}:*` and delete (or maintain a set `sessions:{user_id}` for O(1) lookup)\n\n### Company Context Switching (Members)\n\n- Member calls `POST /users/me/company` with `company_id`\n- Updates `company_id` in Redis session\n- Next access token includes new company context\n\n## API Endpoints\n\n### OAuth\n\n| Method | Path                       | Description                    | Auth     |\n|--------|----------------------------|--------------------------------|----------|\n| GET    | /auth/github               | Redirect to GitHub OAuth       | Public   |\n| GET    | /auth/github/callback      | Handle GitHub callback         | Public   |\n| GET    | /auth/google               | Redirect to Google OAuth       | Public   |\n| GET    | /auth/google/callback      | Handle Google callback         | Public   |\n| POST   | /auth/refresh              | Refresh access token           | Cookie   |\n| POST   | /auth/logout               | Destroy session                | Auth     |\n| POST   | /auth/link                 | Link OAuth to existing user    | Auth     |\n\n### Signup\n\n| Method | Path                       | Description                    | Auth     |\n|--------|----------------------------|--------------------------------|----------|\n| POST   | /auth/signup/company       | Create company after OAuth     | Auth     |\n\n### User\n\n| Method | Path                       | Description                    | Auth     |\n|--------|----------------------------|--------------------------------|----------|\n| GET    | /users/me                  | Current user + active company  | Auth     |\n| POST   | /users/me/company          | Switch active company context  | Auth     |\n\n### Company\n\n| Method | Path                       | Description                    | Auth     |\n|--------|----------------------------|--------------------------------|----------|\n| GET    | /companies/:id             | Company details                | Auth     |\n\n### Invitations (Admin only)\n\n| Method | Path                              | Description              | Auth     |\n|--------|-----------------------------------|--------------------------|----------|\n| POST   | /companies/:id/invitations        | Invite engineer by email | Admin    |\n| GET    | /companies/:id/invitations        | List pending invitations | Admin    |\n| DELETE | /companies/:id/invitations/:invId | Revoke invitation        | Admin    |\n| GET    | /invitations/accept?token=xxx     | Accept invite → OAuth    | Public   |\n\n## Module Architecture\n\n```\nbackend/src/modules/\n├── auth/\n│   ├── auth.module.ts\n│   ├── auth.controller.ts\n│   ├── auth.service.ts\n│   ├── strategies/\n│   │   ├── github.strategy.ts      # Arctic GitHub provider\n│   │   └── google.strategy.ts      # Arctic Google provider\n│   ├── guards/\n│   │   ├── auth.guard.ts           # JWT + session validation\n│   │   └── roles.guard.ts          # Role-based access\n│   ├── decorators/\n│   │   ├── current-user.decorator.ts\n│   │   ├── roles.decorator.ts\n│   │   └── public.decorator.ts\n│   ├── dto/\n│   ├── types/\n│   └── constants/\n├── session/\n│   ├── session.module.ts\n│   ├── session.service.ts\n│   └── types/\n├── user/\n│   ├── user.module.ts\n│   ├── user.service.ts\n│   ├── user.model.ts\n│   ├── dto/\n│   └── types/\n├── oauth-account/\n│   ├── oauth-account.module.ts\n│   ├── oauth-account.service.ts\n│   ├── oauth-account.model.ts\n│   ├── dto/\n│   └── types/\n├── company/\n│   ├── company.module.ts\n│   ├── company.service.ts\n│   ├── company.model.ts\n│   ├── dto/\n│   └── types/\n├── invitation/\n│   ├── invitation.module.ts\n│   ├── invitation.service.ts\n│   ├── invitation.model.ts\n│   ├── dto/\n│   └── types/\n├── cache/\n│   ├── cache.module.ts              # ioredis read/write + redlock\n│   └── cache.service.ts\n└── db/\n    └── db.module.ts                 # Knex pg read/write connections\n```\n\n## Security\n\n### OAuth\n\n- CSRF protection via `state` parameter (Arctic handles this)\n- PKCE for Google (Arctic native support)\n- OAuth access tokens encrypted at rest (AES-256) in `oauth_accounts.access_token`\n\n### JWT\n\n- Signed with HS256 (symmetric, single backend)\n- Access token payload: `{ user_id, session_id, role, company_id }` — minimal claims\n- Refresh token: opaque UUID stored alongside session in Redis (not a JWT)\n\n### Transport\n\n- Refresh token: `httpOnly`, `secure`, `sameSite: strict` cookie\n- Access token: response body, stored in memory on frontend (not localStorage)\n- CORS: restricted to frontend domain\n\n### Rate Limiting\n\n- `/auth/*` endpoints: 10 req/min per IP (`@nestjs/throttler`)\n- Token refresh: 5 req/min per user\n\n### Invitation Tokens\n\n- Cryptographically random (48 bytes, base64url)\n- 72hr expiry\n- Single use — `accepted_at` set on use\n\n## Infrastructure Dependencies\n\n### PostgreSQL\n\n- Write primary + read replica(s)\n- Knex.js via `nest-knexjs` with named connections (`KNEX_READ_CONNECTION`, `KNEX_WRITE_CONNECTION`)\n- Client: `pg`\n\n### Redis\n\n- Write primary + read replica\n- ioredis with read/write separation (replicating recommendations-service pattern)\n- Keyv for cache abstraction\n- Redlock for distributed locking\n- Session TTL: 30 days\n\n### NPM Packages (New)\n\n- `arctic` — OAuth 2.0 provider helpers\n- `@nestjs/jwt` — JWT signing/verification\n- `pg` — PostgreSQL client\n- `knex` + `nest-knexjs` — query builder + NestJS integration\n- `ioredis` — Redis client\n- `@keyv/redis` — Keyv Redis adapter\n- `redlock` — distributed locking\n- `@nestjs/throttler` — rate limiting\n\n## Patterns (Following recommendations-service)\n\n### Database Layer\n\n- Knex read/write connection separation\n- Soft deletes (`.whereNull('deleted_at')` on all reads)\n- UUID primary keys via `randomUUID()` from `node:crypto`\n- Timestamps via `knex.fn.now()`\n- Input/output DTO validation via `plainToInstance()` + `validate()`\n- Thin service layer delegating to models\n- Table name constants (never raw strings)\n- Migrations wrapped in transactions, idempotent\n\n### Redis/Cache Layer\n\n- ioredis read/write clients with health-based fallback\n- Keyv cache managers with namespace isolation\n- Redlock for distributed locking\n- Recent-writes tracking for read-after-write consistency\n"
}

Now I have a thorough understanding of the design spec and the existing codebase context. Here is my review.

---

# Auth Design Spec Review

**File reviewed:** `/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/no-name-proj/docs/superpowers/specs/2026-03-22-auth-design.md`

---

## What is done well

- Clean separation of concerns across modules (auth, session, user, company, invitation, cache, db).
- Token exchange pattern (temporary auth code from callback, not raw JWTs in the URL) is a solid security choice.
- Refresh token rotation with opaque UUIDs stored in Redis is correct practice.
- Session-backed JWT validation (checking Redis on every request) gives immediate revocation capability, which is critical for B2B.
- The data model is normalized sensibly: `oauth_accounts` separated from `users`, `company_members` as a join table.
- Following existing recommendations-service patterns for Knex read/write separation and ioredis is good for consistency.
- Invitation tokens: 48-byte cryptographically random, 72hr expiry, single-use -- all sound.

---

## CRITICAL

### C1. Dual/conflicting role and company modeling on `users` table

The `users` table has both a `role` column (admin/member) and a `company_id` FK. Simultaneously, `company_members` has its own `role` column per membership. This creates ambiguity:

- Which `role` is authoritative -- `users.role` or `company_members.role`?
- The spec says `company_id` on `users` is "the admin's owned company, NULL for pure members." But the session stores a single `role` and `company_id`, and members can switch companies via `POST /users/me/company`. When a member switches companies, what role do they have? The `users.role` says "member", but nothing stops the `company_members.role` from differing.
- If a user is an admin of Company A and gets invited as a member to Company B (the spec says admins are 1:1 with a company, but there is no DB constraint preventing an admin from also appearing in `company_members` for another company), the `users.role` would say "admin" while their `company_members` entry for Company B says "member."

**Recommendation:** Remove `role` and `company_id` from the `users` table entirely. Derive role from `company_members` based on the active company context. The session already stores `company_id` and `role` -- these should come from `company_members` at login/context-switch time, not from a static column on `users`. This eliminates the single source of truth problem.

### C2. No database-level constraint for admin 1:1 company enforcement

The spec states "Admins: 1:1 with company (enforced in app logic)." App-logic-only enforcement of a critical invariant in a multi-tenant system is dangerous. Concurrent requests or bugs in the service layer could create multiple admins per company or one admin across multiple companies.

**Recommendation:** Add a partial unique index on `company_members`: `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON company_members(company_id) WHERE role = 'admin' AND deleted_at IS NULL`. This guarantees at the database level that each company has at most one admin. Also consider using Redlock during admin assignment as a belt-and-suspenders approach.

### C3. Token exchange flow is underspecified and potentially broken

The spec describes two contradictory flows:

1. Section "OAuth Login Sequence" step 8 says: "Return JWT (access + refresh) to frontend."
2. Section "Token Exchange Security" says: "OAuth callback returns a temporary authorization code to frontend (not JWT). Frontend exchanges code for JWT via POST."

But there is no `POST /auth/token` or `POST /auth/exchange` endpoint in the API table. The callback endpoints (`GET /auth/{provider}/callback`) are listed, but no exchange endpoint exists. The actual mechanism for the frontend to receive tokens is undefined.

**Recommendation:** Add a `POST /auth/token` (or `POST /auth/exchange`) endpoint to the API table. Clarify the full sequence: callback stores a short-lived code in Redis, redirects to frontend with the code as a query parameter, frontend POSTs the code to the exchange endpoint, backend returns access token in body and sets refresh token as httpOnly cookie.

---

## MAJOR

### M1. Refresh token storage location is not defined

The spec says refresh tokens are "opaque UUID stored alongside session in Redis" but the session structure does not include a `refresh_token` field. Where exactly is the refresh token stored? Is it a separate Redis key? Is it a field in the session hash? The refresh flow says "validates refresh token, checks session exists" but does not explain the mapping between a refresh token and a session.

**Recommendation:** Either add `refresh_token` to the session structure, or define a separate Redis key pattern like `refresh:{token_uuid} -> session:{user_id}:{session_id}`. Also specify that on rotation, the old refresh token must be invalidated atomically (using a Redis transaction or Redlock) to prevent race conditions with concurrent refresh requests.

### M2. Account linking flow is vague and has no resolution endpoint

Step 6 of the OAuth flow mentions "prompt to link" for two cases, but the actual UX/API flow is unclear:

- What does "prompt to link" mean in a server-side OAuth callback? The backend cannot show a UI prompt.
- `POST /auth/link` exists but requires Auth -- the user is not yet authenticated at this point in the flow (they are trying to log in via a new provider).
- How does the backend communicate to the frontend that linking is needed? What temporary state is stored? How does the frontend confirm the link?

**Recommendation:** Define the linking flow explicitly. For example: backend detects the linking case, stores a temporary linking token in Redis, redirects to frontend with a linking prompt URL. The user then authenticates with their existing provider, and the frontend calls `POST /auth/link` with the linking token + existing session. Alternatively, auto-link when the email matches (simpler, but has security implications if email is not verified by the provider).

### M3. `invitations.role` allows admin invitations, contradicting the admin model

The `invitations` table has `role: ENUM(admin, member)`. But the spec says admins create companies themselves via `POST /auth/signup/company` and have a 1:1 relationship. If you can invite someone as an admin, you would have two admins for one company, or you would be transferring ownership -- neither of which is described.

**Recommendation:** Either restrict `invitations.role` to `member` only, or define an explicit ownership transfer flow. If the intent is future flexibility, document it as such but implement the constraint now.

### M4. No PKCE for GitHub OAuth

The spec calls out "PKCE for Google (Arctic native support)" but does not mention PKCE for GitHub. GitHub has supported PKCE since 2021. Without PKCE, the GitHub flow is vulnerable to authorization code interception attacks, especially on mobile or when the redirect URI is not perfectly locked down.

**Recommendation:** Enable PKCE for GitHub as well. Arctic supports it.

### M5. Missing `invited_by` on invitations table

There is no record of which admin created an invitation. This is important for audit trails in a B2B app and for debugging when customers report issues.

**Recommendation:** Add `invited_by UUID FK -> users._id NOT NULL` to the `invitations` table.

### M6. No "logout all sessions" API endpoint

The session operations section describes "Destroy all" (scan/delete all sessions for a user), but there is no corresponding API endpoint. This is an important security feature for B2B (e.g., when an employee leaves or credentials are compromised).

**Recommendation:** Add `POST /auth/logout/all` to the API table.

---

## MINOR

### m1. Session `company_id` for admins is redundant but could desync

For admins, `company_id` is derivable from the user record (or from `company_members`). Storing it in the session creates a cache that could become stale if, for example, company deletion happens but sessions are not invalidated. Members have the same risk when they are removed from a company.

**Recommendation:** Document the invalidation strategy: when a user is removed from a company or a company is deleted, all relevant sessions must be destroyed. Consider adding a note about this to the "Destroy all" session operation.

### m2. `GET /companies/:id` authorization is too broad

The endpoint is listed as "Auth" (any authenticated user). In a multi-tenant B2B app, a user should only be able to view companies they belong to.

**Recommendation:** Add authorization: the user must be a member of the company (check `company_members`), or restrict to the user's active company context.

### m3. Rate limiting for invitation endpoints is not specified

`/auth/*` has rate limits, but invitation creation (`POST /companies/:id/invitations`) does not. An admin could accidentally or maliciously spam invitations.

**Recommendation:** Add rate limiting for invitation creation (e.g., 20 per hour per company).

### m4. No `UNIQUE(company_id, email)` on invitations

The same email could be invited to the same company multiple times (creating duplicate pending invitations), which would confuse admins and waste invitation tokens.

**Recommendation:** Add a unique constraint on `(company_id, email)` where `accepted_at IS NULL AND deleted_at IS NULL`.

### m5. `oauth_accounts.email` purpose is unclear

The `oauth_accounts` table has an `email` field separate from `users.email`. It is not clear when these would differ or how conflicts are resolved. If the user changes their email at the OAuth provider, which email is canonical?

**Recommendation:** Add a note clarifying that `oauth_accounts.email` captures the provider-reported email at link time, `users.email` is the canonical app email, and describe whether/how provider email changes are synced.

### m6. HS256 JWT signing

HS256 (symmetric) works for a single backend, as noted. However, if the architecture ever scales to multiple services that need to verify tokens (e.g., a separate websocket service, a frontend SSR server), HS256 requires sharing the secret with all verifiers.

**Recommendation:** This is fine for now but note it as a future consideration. If multi-service verification is anticipated, RS256/ES256 with a public key would be more appropriate.

---

## SUGGESTIONS

### S1. Consider a `users_companies` view or helper

Since the role/company relationship should be derived from `company_members`, consider creating a database view or a Knex query helper that joins `users` with `company_members` for the active company. This will be reused heavily.

### S2. Add `last_login_at` to users

Useful for admin dashboards and security auditing. Can be updated on session creation.

### S3. Define the signup flow for invited members more explicitly

The spec has `GET /invitations/accept?token=xxx` which "Accept invite -> OAuth", but the full flow is unclear. Does accepting an invitation redirect to OAuth? What if the user already has an account? What if they authenticate with a different email than the invitation was sent to? Spell out the branching logic.

### S4. Consider refresh token family/lineage tracking

When rotating refresh tokens, if a previously-rotated (now-invalid) refresh token is reused, it likely indicates token theft. A common pattern is to track refresh token families and invalidate the entire family (all sessions) when reuse is detected.

### S5. Module architecture does not show a `company-member` module

The `company_members` table is in the data model but there is no corresponding module in the architecture tree. Clarify whether `company.service.ts` owns the membership CRUD or if a separate module is planned.

---

## Summary

The design is well-structured overall and makes solid choices on token handling, session management, and multi-provider OAuth. The three critical issues -- the dual role/company modeling ambiguity (C1), lack of DB-level admin uniqueness enforcement (C2), and the underspecified token exchange flow (C3) -- should be resolved before implementation begins, as they affect the core data model and primary authentication flow. The major issues (M1-M6) are important for security and completeness but can be addressed during implementation if needed.

**Critical fixes needed:**

1. **C1** — Remove `role` and `company_id` from `users` table. Derive from `company_members` based on active company context.
2. **C2** — Add partial unique index: one admin per company at DB level.
3. **C3** — Add `POST /auth/token` exchange endpoint, clarify the full callback → exchange flow.

**Major fixes:**

4. **M1** — Define refresh token storage: separate Redis key `refresh:{token} → session:{user_id}:{session_id}`
5. **M2** — Spell out the linking flow with temporary linking token
6. **M3** — Restrict `invitations.role` to `member` only for now
7. **M4** — PKCE for GitHub too
8. **M5** — Add `invited_by` to invitations
9. **M6** — Add `POST /auth/logout/all` endpoint

**Minor fixes:** m1-m6 and suggestions S1-S5 — all valid, incorporating.
